## Reasoning

The champion scores -4.20, far better than all others. Let me analyze it:
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=74) with 4 transitions: neighbor-below-8, perceive-yellow, gray-floor, neighbor-below-1
- State 1: GoToColor (clr=2=green) with 3 transitions: neighbor-below-8, black-floor, black-floor

The key insight: The robot explores, then goes toward GREEN (the beneficial source), and returns when it detects black floor (it's at source). The champion seems to be collecting from the green side (which decreases score = good).

Wait - let me re-read: "an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1." So green walls → one type, blue walls → other type. The champion uses clr=2 (green) to navigate to the source.

Strategy:
1. **Survival**: Copy champion exactly
2. **Exploitation**: Small variations on champion topology (2-state, explore + GoToColor)
   - Vary rwm, clr (try blue=3 vs green=2), probabilities
   - Try GoToColor with clr=1 (red wall for nest navigation)
3. **Exploration**: 3-state machines with explicit return-to-nest behavior

Key observations:
- clr colors: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan
- The champion goes toward green (clr=2). Green walls are behind one half of source.
- Need to also consider returning to nest (white floor = nest, red wall = nest side)

Let me design machines:

**Exploitation of champion:**
- Copy with minor tweaks (rwm, probabilities)
- Try clr=1 (go toward red/nest wall) instead of green in state 1 - might help return
- Try 3-state: explore → go-to-green → go-to-red(return)

**Exploration:**
- 3-state: explore → GoToColor(green source) → GoToColor(red nest)
- 4-state versions with more sophisticated logic

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The top 8 machines all score around -3.8 to -4.28, with a huge gap to rank 9 (-0.95). The 2-state machines dominate the top. The champion structure is clear:
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=74) with 4 transitions all pointing back to state 1 (GoToColor green)
- State 1: GoToColor(clr=2=green) with 3 transitions all pointing back to state 0

Key observations:
1. All top machines are 2-state with the same basic topology
2. The champion has all transitions in state 0 pointing to state 1 (destination index 0 = the other state)
3. State 1 transitions back to state 0 on: neighbor-below-8, black-floor, black-floor
4. The 3-state machines with a GoToColor(red) return state perform poorly (-0.76 to 1.76)

The critical question: why do 3-state machines fail? They seem to get confused between returning and exploring. The 2-state champion works because: explore until seeing yellow or gray floor or few neighbors → go to green source → when at black floor or few neighbors → explore again.

Wait - I notice the champion's state 0 has transitions pointing to destination "3" in the original but in round 2 they all point to "0" (which means state 1 since the only other state). The champion works as: explore → GoToGreen → explore (cycling).

The black floor condition in state 1 (p=0.0865 and p=0.2499) means robots leave the green source with low probability each step, spending time collecting. This is good.

**Strategy for round 3:**
1. **Survival**: Copy champion exactly
2. **Exploitation**: Fine-tune parameters around champion
   - Try rwm=70, 75, 78, 85
   - Try w0x0 variations (6.0, 7.0, 8.0)
   - Try p0x1 variations (0.10, 0.13, 0.15)
   - Try p1x1/p1x2 variations
   - Try adding a white-floor condition in state 1 (to detect nest and return)
3. **Exploration**: Try clr=3 (blue) - maybe blue walls are the beneficial ones
   - Try 2-state with GoToColor(blue) instead of green

## Reasoning

Key findings from round 3:
1. **clr=3 (blue) is catastrophic** (+12.53) - green (clr=2) is the correct beneficial source
2. The champion has lower p1x1=0.05 and p1x2=0.15 (less likely to leave source) - spending more time collecting is better
3. All top machines are 2-state with same topology
4. Parameters converging around: rwm0=74, w0x0=6.8644, p0x1=0.1174, p0x2=0.953, p0x3=1, w0x3=17.4036, w1x0=10.5944

The champion differs from round 2 champion mainly in: p1x0=7→7 (neighbor threshold), p1x1=0.0865→0.05, p1x2=0.2499→0.15. Lower exit probabilities from source = better.

**Direction**: Keep exploring lower exit probabilities (p1x1, p1x2). Also try:
- Even lower p1x1 (0.03, 0.04) and p1x2 (0.10, 0.12)
- Vary w1x0 (neighbor threshold steepness in state 1)
- Try p1x0=6 (lower neighbor threshold to leave source)
- Try adding white-floor condition in state 1 (to leave nest area quickly)
- Vary w0x0 slightly

The key insight: robots should stay at the green source longer (collect more items), so lower p1x1 and p1x2 are better. But they also need to return to nest - the black floor condition triggers departure from source naturally since black=source floor.

Wait - black floor IS the source. So c0=black triggers transition FROM explore TO GoToGreen. In state 1 (GoToGreen), black floor conditions mean "I'm at the source, leave with probability p". Lower p = stay longer = more items collected per visit... but then how do they return to nest? They must just wander back via exploration.

Let me focus exploitation on lower p1x1/p1x2 and some parameter sweeps.

## Reasoning

The champion (-4.49) still holds from round 3. In round 5, no machine beat it. Key observations:

1. **2-state machines dominate** - 3-state machines consistently underperform (-0.59 to -0.78)
2. **The champion parameters are near-optimal**: rwm0=74, w0x0=6.8644, p0x1=0.1174, p0x2=0.953, p0x3=1, w0x3=17.4036, p1x0=7, w1x0=10.5944, p1x1=0.05, p1x2=0.15
3. The best round 5 result (-4.20) added a white-floor condition (c=2, p=0.95) to state 1 - this allows robots to leave state 1 when they detect white floor (nest), which is good
4. The second best (-4.15) uses white-floor condition as exit from source state (c=2, p=0.9) + small black floor prob (p=0.05)

**New hypothesis**: The white-floor condition in state 1 (c1x=2, p=0.95) is actually helpful - it means when robots reach the nest (white floor), they quickly switch back to exploration. This creates a complete foraging loop.

**Strategy for round 6**:
1. Keep champion
2. Combine the white-floor exit (from rank 1 round 5) with lower p1x1/p1x2
3. Try the white-floor pattern from rank 2 (c=2, p=0.9 + c=0, p=0.05)
4. Fine-tune w0x0, p0x2, w0x3 parameters
5. Try varying p0x3 (neighbor-below threshold count=1 vs 2)
6. Explore: what if we use c=5 (random probability) in state 1 for departure?

The white-floor exit in state 1 seems promising - when robot is at nest (white floor) with an item, it should go back to explore. Let me build around this.

## Reasoning

The champion improved slightly to -4.51. Key findings from round 6:

**Champion structure** (state 1):
- c1x0: neighbor-below-7 (w=10.5944) → leave source if too crowded
- c1x1: black-floor (p=0.05) → small chance to leave black floor
- c1x2: **white-floor (p=0.9)** → high chance to leave when at nest (WHITE floor)

This is the key insight: the white-floor condition in state 1 means robots quickly leave GoToGreen state when they detect white floor (nest area), returning to exploration. This creates an efficient loop: explore → go to green source (collect) → when at nest (white floor), switch back to explore.

The second-best machine adds a gray-floor (c=1, p=0.9) condition too, giving a 4-transition state 1.

**Convergent parameters**: All top machines share the same state 0 parameters exactly. The variation is in state 1 exit conditions.

**Strategy for round 7**:
1. Keep champion
2. Exploit: vary p1x2 (white-floor probability: 0.85, 0.92, 0.95, 1.0), p1x1 (black-floor: 0.03, 0.04, 0.06), p1x0 (neighbor threshold: 6, 8)
3. Combine: white-floor + small black-floor + neighbor condition variations
4. Try: adding gray-floor exit from state 1 (when on gray = traveling, go back to explore)
5. Explore: try w1x0 variations (9, 11, 12) with white-floor exit

The champion uses exactly: neighbor-below-7 (steepness 10.5944) + black(0.05) + white(0.9). Let me fine-tune around this.

## Reasoning

Champion improved to -4.70. The key change: **p1x0=6** (neighbor-below threshold lowered from 7 to 6) with the white-floor exit (p1x2=0.9). 

Key observations from round 7:
1. Champion: p1x0=6 (neighbor threshold), p1x1=0.05 (black floor), p1x2=0.9 (white floor) - this is the winning combo
2. The 2-state machine without neighbor condition in state 1 (-1.49) is terrible - neighbor condition is crucial
3. p1x0=7 machines all cluster around -4.26 to -4.42, worse than champion's p1x0=6
4. p1x2=0.95 is slightly worse than 0.9; p1x2=0.85 also worse
5. Adding gray-floor exit (c=1) helps slightly when combined correctly

**New direction**: Try p1x0=5 (even lower neighbor threshold), and try p1x0=4. Also try:
- Vary w1x0 (steepness): 8, 9, 11, 12 with p1x0=6
- Try p1x1=0.04, 0.03 with p1x0=6
- Try p1x2=0.88, 0.92 with p1x0=6
- Try adding 4th transition to champion (gray floor, or another neighbor condition)
- Try p1x0=5 with w1x0=10.5944

The champion's neighbor-below-6 means: if fewer than 6 neighbors are within range, switch back to exploration. This disperses robots from the crowded source area.

## Reasoning

The champion (-4.70) still holds from round 7. Round 8 best was -4.42 (p1x0=4). The scores are noisy - same machine scored -3.80 and -4.70 in different rounds. 

Key findings:
1. p1x0=4 scored -4.42 (best this round) vs p1x0=6 (champion at -4.70)
2. p1x0=5 scored -4.07 to -4.16
3. p1x0=6 scored -3.80 this round (but -4.70 as champion)
4. The stochasticity is high - scores vary ~0.5-1.0 between runs

The trend in neighbor threshold: 6→4 seems to improve. Let me try p1x0=3, p1x0=2, p1x0=1.

Also: w1x0 (steepness) hasn't been varied much with p1x0=4. Try w1x0 variations with p1x0=4.

The p1x1=0.05 (black floor exit) and p1x2=0.9 (white floor exit) seem stable. Let me try p1x1=0.03, 0.04 with p1x0=4.

For exploration, I should try something more different - maybe varying state 0 parameters that haven't been touched (p0x0=8 threshold, w0x0=6.8644).

Also: the 3-state machine scored -1.07, confirming 2-state is better.

**Plan**:
- 1x champion (exact copy)
- 4x exploitation around p1x0=4 (vary p1x1, p1x2, w1x0)
- 3x try p1x0=3, 2, 1
- 2x try combinations of p1x0=4 with 4 transitions
- 2x try varying state 0 parameters (w0x0, p0x2) with champion-like state 1
- 1x exploration: different topology

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
